[02:26:51] When a new user is created we have to assign a password to that ID to give to the new user so they can log in
[02:26:59] It's common to ask the new user to change their password when they first log in to force a user to change their password on the next login
[02:27:08] And this is not just for the first login, but anytime we want that user to update their password,
[02:27:14] we would type pass WD dash dash expire and then the user ID.
[02:27:19] Or we can type Sudo CH-Age dash-last-day, 1970-01-01, and then the user ID.
[02:27:28] Or we could type Sudo C-H-Age dash-last day, zero, then the user ID.
[02:27:36] And then the next time the user ID logs in, they will be asked to change their password.
[02:27:40] An example of this would be for test ad user. You'd type pass WD dash dash expire test ad user or
[02:27:48] pseudo CH dash dash last day 1970.01.01 test ad user or Sudo CH dash dash last day.
[02:27:59] 0 test ad user.

[02:51:42] They're not in the group, but they do have access to this file.

[02:51:54] but they can execute a file.

[02:56:37] and directory in that directory, we would need to add the uppercase R option for recursive.
[02:56:43] I'll type Chmod, uppercase R for recursively apply these permissions.
[02:56:50] We'll give the user owner, read, write, and execute permissions.
[02:56:56] The group permissions will be read and execute, and then for everyone else, we will give them read permissions.
[02:57:05] I will give this the directory name, new directory.
[02:57:10] And typing LS dash LH shows us that the new directory permissions are now set that way.
[02:57:17] Setting the privileges this way is called alphabetic notation or symbolic notation.
[02:57:23] They're the same thing depending on who you're talking to.
[02:57:25] It is when we can see the letters or the symbols.
[02:57:29] The other notation for setting privileges is called octal notation.
[02:57:34] We still use the same read, write, and execute for user owner, group and other,
[02:57:39] but we use a single number to set the user owner privileges,
[02:57:43] a different number to set the group privileges, and a third number for the privileges of other.
[02:57:50] How this works is read, write, and execute each have a value assigned to them.
[02:57:56] Read has a value of four, write has a value of two, and execute has a value of one.
[02:58:02] No privileges has a value of 0.
[02:58:04] If we have these up 4 plus 2 plus 1, we get 7, the highest number we can have.
[02:58:11] But we can also use 0.
[02:58:13] So we have 8 different numbers we can use for the permissions, and that's where we get the name Octal permissions.
[02:58:20] Let's look at an example.
[02:58:22] In this case, the user owner has read, write, and execute privileges.
[02:58:28] So we have 4 plus 2 plus 1 and we get 7.
[02:58:32] So the user owner octal privileges is 7.
[02:58:36] The group privileges are read and execute.
[02:58:40] 4 plus 1 gives us the octal number of 5.
[02:58:44] And the other only has read privileges, so their octal number is 4.
[02:58:49] Then we take that same Chmod to change mode of the user privileges with those 3 numbers.
[02:58:56] Chmod 754 nanophile.TXT.
[02:59:02] LS-LH.
[02:59:04] And we see we updated the privileges using the octal notation this time.
[02:59:09] If we want to give the user owner full privileges and no privileges for the group or other,
[02:59:16] I would Chimod the file permissions to be 7-0.0.
[02:59:22] And I'll do that with Chmod 7-00 nanofile.t.
[02:59:30] LS-LH.
[02:59:33] And now only the user owner has privileges for this file.
[02:59:37] If you are working on a website or something connected to the internet,
[02:59:41] it's usually a bad idea to give the privileges of 777.
[02:59:45] Although you may need to at times, at least temporarily,
[02:59:49] you should still be careful when and where you do that.
[02:59:52] If you have a website with 777 privileges available to the general public,
[02:59:56] you are inviting hackers not only to add malicious...
[03:00:00] but also they can execute that malicious code.
[03:00:03] In general, if you want the public to have right access to a directory,
[03:00:08] that's okay, but don't give them execute access for that same directory.
[03:00:13] And the same thing for the execute privileges.
[03:00:15] If you want the general public to be able to execute files, that's okay too.
[03:00:19] Just don't give them right privileges for that same directory.

[03:00:30] In the last video we talked about file permissions for the user owner, group, and other.
[03:00:36] We were able to change the file permissions for files we are the user owner of without needing to be a privileged user.
[03:00:45] From here forward I'm going to refer to the user owner as owner.
[03:00:50] Standard Linux users cannot change the file owner.
[03:00:54] But they can change the group for their files, but only to a group of which they are a member.
[03:01:01] You may be thinking, why can't I give my file to someone else?
[03:01:04] It's my file. I should be able to share my files with anyone I like.
[03:01:09] And you can share what's in your files with anyone you like by changing the file permissions as we saw in the last video.
[03:01:18] But you will need to be a privileged user to change the owner of a file.
[03:01:23] There are a couple of reasons why you cannot just change the owner of one of your files or directories to another user ID.
[03:01:31] One reason is you may have a disk quota on your login ID, and if you change the owner of a file,
[03:01:38] that file would no longer count against your disk quota, and it will count against the disk quota of the user ID you just set it to.
[03:01:47] Another reason is someone might put some malicious code in a file and then change the owner of that file to your login ID.
[03:01:55] And then you could be blamed for putting malicious code on the computer.
[03:02:00] So only a privileged user can change the file owner.
[03:02:05] An owner can update the group for a file or directory, he or she is the owner of, but as I mentioned earlier, it is limited to the groups that user ID is a member.

[03:26:41] In the next video, we'll look at more permissions for the Linux command line.
[03:26:45] In this video, we're going to look at the Set UID flag.
[03:26:51] Looking up what the Set UID flag does, we'll find a definition similar to this.
[03:26:56] Set UID is a permissions bit flag.
[03:26:59] When the Set UID bit flag is set, it allows the users to run an executable with the permissions of the file owner.
[03:27:08] And then the most common example people use to explain Set UID is the PassWD command.
[03:27:15] Every user on the system can update their password with the PassWD command.
[03:27:20] The passwords are stored in the Etsy Shadow file, a file owned by the root user,
[03:27:26] so when the standard user runs the PassWD command, they run that command as the Root user,
[03:27:33] which allows them to update their password in the Etsy Shadow file.
[03:27:37] To find out what files being executed when I type PassWD, I'm going to type
[03:27:43] Which PassWD.
[03:27:47] And Linux tells us we're executing the file
[03:27:50] slash USR slash B-I-N slash past WD.
[03:27:56] So when I type past W-D, I'm really saying go to the slash USR-S-BIN directory and run past WD from there.
[03:28:06] To see the permissions of the past W-D file, I will type L-S-L-H-U-S-R-BIN past W-D.
[03:28:18] Here we see an S where we usually see an X or a hyphen for the owner execute permission.
[03:28:25] That S is showing us the Set UID bit is set for the file, user, bin, pass WD.
[03:28:32] The owner of that file is root, so when anyone on the computer executes that file, it is executed as the root user.
[03:28:40] Running this file as the root user allows a standard user to update their password in the Etsy Shadow file.
[03:28:48] a file that only root and privileged users have access to.
[03:28:52] Now that we know the basics of how set UID works,
[03:28:56] let's try and create our own executable file.
[03:29:00] I'm going to clear the screen and I'm going to make a new directory for our set UID test.
[03:29:07] Sudo make DIRSRV set UID.
[03:29:16] Now I'm going into the SRV directory with CD.
[03:29:20] SRV and showing it was created with LS dash LH.
[03:29:25] We see that the owner in the group are now root.
[03:29:29] I'm going to change them both to Michael with Sudo Chon Michael.
[03:29:37] That's set UID.
[03:29:40] And now we see the owner in the group are both Michael.
[03:29:45] And the permissions show Michael has full permissions and everyone else has
[03:29:49] read and execute permissions for this new Set UID directory.

[07:54:10] Before we look at compressing TAR files in a single command,
[07:54:14] there are two other ways of compressing files that I want to look at.
[07:54:18] The first one is called BZip 2. BZip 2 is similar to GZip
[07:54:23] And the idea behind BZp 2 is that it is a different compression method that can create a smaller archive file than the GZip command, but it may take a little longer to run.
[07:54:33] The BZip 2 command follows the same format as GZip.
[07:54:37] To zip a file with BZip 2, we would just type BZip 2 and then the file name we want to zip.
[07:54:44] BZip 2, Mary.TXT.
[07:54:50] We see that the Mary.TXT file is gone.
[07:54:53] and Mary.txt.BZ2 is created.
[07:54:57] BZ2 is the extension for BZP2 files.
[07:55:01] We can cat a BZP2 file with BZCat.
[07:55:05] BZCat, Mary.tT.BZ2.
[07:55:10] We can grep a BZ2 file with BZGREP.
[07:55:14] BZGREP, Lamb, Mary.TT.BZ2.
[07:55:22] and we can decompress a BZp2 file with BZP2-D
[07:55:28] Mary.TXT.BZ2.
[07:55:31] I'm going to zip that back up with BZP2, Mary.TXT,
[07:55:36] because I also want to show that we can unzip a BZP2 file with B, unzip2, Mary.TXT.com.

[08:59:52] Kron works well to schedule jobs that are running on a server or a computer that is running 24 hours a day.
[08:59:59] But a lot of
[09:00:00] computers and laptops are not running 24 hours a day. We turn them off when not in use.
[09:00:05] In our last few videos, we created a cron job to run a backup every Monday morning at 304 a.m.
[09:00:13] If the computer was not powered on at 304 a.m. on Monday morning, that cron would be skipped.
[09:00:19] And maybe missing one backup might not be bad.
[09:00:22] But if that computer is off every Monday at 403 a.m.
[09:00:27] That backup could be skipped for weeks, months, or maybe never run.
[09:00:31] And that could be bad.
[09:00:32] So instead of using Cron to schedule a backup, we may want to use Anacron.
[09:00:37] Anacron can be used to run a command or script just like Cron, but instead of giving it a specific day and time,
[09:00:44] we give it a more general time frame of hourly, daily, weekly, or monthly.
[09:00:50] When the computer is constantly powered on, Anacron will run the Anacron jobs regularly.
[09:00:56] for each time frame of hour, day, week, and month.
[09:01:00] For computers that are turned off and back on,
[09:01:03] when the computer starts up, the Anacron jobs are checked to see if they need to run at Startup.
[09:01:09] We'll look at what Anacron does at Startup later in this video.
[09:01:13] Anacron is a part of Cron, so let's look at the Cron files in the Etsy directory with LS-L-H
[09:01:21] Etsy Cron Asteris.
[09:01:26] The first thing we see is Etsy Cron tab.
[09:01:29] We're going to come back to this later in this video.
[09:01:32] The next thing found is the directory Etsy Cron D.
[09:01:36] The D is for Damon, so the files in this directory are for the Cron Damon.
[09:01:41] We don't want to change anything in this directory, so we're going to leave this directory alone.

[09:01:53] EtsyCron Monthly and EtsyCron Weekly, all these directories work the same way.
[09:01:59] When that time frames Anacron is run, all the scripts in that directory are run.
[09:02:05] For example, when the Daily Anacron runs, all the scripts in the EtsyCron Daily directory are run.
[09:02:13] So if we want to add a script to be run by Anacron on a daily basis, we would add our script to the Etsy Cron Daily directory.

[10:05:07] And the last thing on the top line is the load average with three numbers.
[10:05:12] The number on the left is the current load average.
[10:05:15] The number in the middle is the load average five minutes ago.
[10:05:18] And the number on the right is the load average 15 minutes ago.
[10:05:22] And the idea behind showing us the three load averages is to help us see trends.
[10:05:28] For example, in this screenshot,
[10:05:30] We see 15 minutes ago the load average was very low at .13.
[10:05:35] Then 5 minutes ago the load average went up to 0.35 and currently the load average is .72.
[10:05:43] This tells us something in the last 10 minutes has put a bit of a load on the system.
[10:05:48] If those load average numbers were the other way around, with the 15 minutes ago being the highest load average
[10:05:55] and the current load average being the lowest,
[10:05:57] we would think that whatever is causing that load is not a problem anymore.
[10:06:02] Which brings us to the next question.
[10:06:03] When is a high load average a problem or what load average number is a problem for the computer?
[10:06:10] And the answer is it depends on the number of CPUs on that computer.
[10:06:15] When we see a load average of 1.00, that means one CPU is processing everything it can.
[10:06:23] It is 100% used.
[10:06:25] On a computer with a single CPU, a load of 1.00 is able to process everything at that moment and nothing has to wait to run.
[10:06:35] If that load hits 1.01 or higher, that load is now more than a single CPU can keep up with,
[10:06:42] and some processes now have to wait to run.
[10:06:46] But if we have a load average of 1.00 and the computer has two CPUs,
[10:06:51] A load average of 1.00 is only using half of the total CPU resources.
[10:06:57] So a load average of 1.00 is not a problem for a computer with two or more CPUs.
[10:07:03] But if that load gets higher than 2.00 for a computer with two CPUs,
[10:07:09] then we have more processes trying to run than we have CPU resources for.
[10:07:14] And some processes will have to wait to run.
[10:07:18] Today, most computers have more than one CPU.
[10:07:21] So a load average of 1.00 is rarely a problem.
[10:07:25] Going back to our terminal, Top is an interactive program,
[10:07:29] meaning we can press a key and change the view we're looking at.

[10:08:33] and every day if more zombie processes are created, you have the potential of running out of process IDs.
[10:08:41] Now don't worry too much about that happening.
[10:08:43] There are more than enough process IDs and the process IDs are recycled,
[10:08:48] but if you have many zombie processes being created regularly and the computer has never restarted,

[14:43:59] And when I say the beginning, I mean when we power on the computer.
[14:44:03] When we power on a computer,
[14:44:05] the motherboard on that computer has a chip with some software on it called
[14:44:10] BIOS. BIOS stands for basic input output system. And it does things like test the memory,
[14:44:17] sets the system clock. It also configures the keyboard, mouse, and other hardware attached to the computer.
[14:44:23] After it does all that, it looks for a drive with a bootloader on it. And the bootloader,
[14:44:29] in our case, is going to have an MS DOS partition table or GPT partition table. That brings us
[14:44:36] to the two main types of BIOS on the motherboard.
[14:44:39] Legacy BIOS and UEFI BIOS.
[14:44:43] Now I know some of you are thinking, it's not UEFI BIOS.
[14:44:46] It's just UEFI.
[14:44:48] I understand that, but for the sake of this explanation,
[14:44:51] I'm calling it UEFI BIOS.
[14:44:55] And Legacy BIOS is called Legacy BIOS or Legacy Boot on UEFI computers.
[14:45:02] On all the other computers, it's just called BIOS.
[14:45:05] Bios, now known as Legacy Bios, legacy boot, came first.
[14:45:09] So we'll start with that one.
[14:45:11] Bios was created in the 1980s and would boot into an MS DOS partition table.
[14:45:17] The MS DOS partition table is also known as the MBR partition table.
[14:45:22] MBR is short for Master Boot Record.
[14:45:26] It's called the Master Boot Record because there is a 1 megabyte boot sector on the hard drive for the partition table information.
[14:45:34] and that would be the master boot record that one master boot sector could get corrupt and you would
[14:45:40] need to restore it or all the data on that disk would be lost the ms dOS mBR partition table has a
[14:45:47] limit of two terabytes which in the 1980s was huge but today hard drives with more than two
[14:45:53] terabytes of storage is common and the ms dOS mbr partition table can only use two terabytes of that
[14:46:02] The MS-DOS MBR partition table runs in a 16-bit processor mode.
[14:46:08] Around 2006, EFI was developed.
[14:46:12] EFI stands for Extensible Firmware Interface.
[14:46:16] Shortly after that came UEFI, Unified Extensible Firmware Interface.
[14:46:22] UEFI uses the GPT partition table.
[14:46:27] GPT stands for the GUID partition table.
[14:46:31] So the PT is for partition table, and the GUID stands for globally unique identifier.
[14:46:39] With the globally unique identifier added, we can now add secure boot,
[14:46:44] which can ensure that only trusted software can be executed on our system.
[14:46:50] We are no longer limited to a one megabyte disk sector.
[14:46:54] The GPT partition table is copied redundantly on the disk, so if one area is corrupt,
[14:47:00] the disk can still be red. The limit is now 9.4 zeta bytes, which is about the size of the entire internet today.
[14:47:10] It can run in 32-bit or 64-bit mode. The UEFI BIOS can handle a lot more hardware details for the CPU, memory, storage, and fans.
[14:47:21] It also supports newer technologies like USB 3.0, NVME, and PCIs. UEFI is backwards compatible,
[14:47:30] so secure boot can be turned off and operating systems created before uEFI can be loaded on a uEFI computer

[15:36:57] When using LVM, there are three parts of the logical volume manager
[15:37:02] that we should be familiar with.
[15:37:04] They are the PV physical volume, the VG,
[15:37:09] which is the volume group sometimes referred to as the virtual group and then there's the lv which is the logical volume we will see those initials are often used with lvm so that's why i'm introducing them to you at the beginning of this video let's start with the p v physical volume first physical volumes are hard drives i'm showing a picture of usb drives here to show that we are talking about actual physical hard drives
[15:37:38] They can be USB drives, internal drives, SSD drives, any physical hard drive.
[15:37:44] And notice that the SDC drive has two partitions.
[15:37:48] With LVM, we can use the full device drives like SDB and SDD, or partitions like SDC1 and SDC2.
[15:37:58] We will have to run a command to make that physical disk a usable PV physical volume for LVM.
[15:38:05] We'll do that in a moment.
[15:38:07] Right now, we're going to continue on to the second thing I said we needed to be familiar with for LVM,
[15:38:12] and that is the VG or volume group. In this picture, we can see that the blue area represents a volume
[15:38:20] group, which is made up of four drives, or five partitions. The SDC has two partitions.
[15:38:27] You can think of a volume group as a virtual hard drive. That's why VG is sometimes referred to as a
[15:38:34] virtual group.
[15:38:35] Because it's not a real hard drive, but Linux will recognize this group as a single device.
[15:38:41] So we can have multiple physical volumes used to create a single volume group.
[15:38:47] And then we can add more physical volumes or remove them from a volume group as needed.
[15:38:52] The third thing we need for LVM is an LV, a logical volume.
[15:38:58] And the logical volume works like a partition, the way a volume group works like a hard drive.
[15:39:03] In this picture, the orange is meant to represent a logical volume on the blue volume group,

[16:37:53] the read-right speed is good.
[16:37:56] It is easy to set up.
[16:37:58] It is easy to expand the size.
[16:38:01] The size can also be reduced,
[16:38:03] but that was not shown in my tutorials
[16:38:05] because there is a chance of data loss.
[16:38:09] When adding disks,
[16:38:10] disks do not need to be the same size as the original disk,
[16:38:14] and all the disk space can be used.
[16:38:17] The disadvantage of using a linear logical volume
[16:38:21] is there is no redundancy.
[16:38:24] So if the drive fails, all the data is lost.
[16:38:27] And if the linear logical volume is using multiple disks,
[16:38:31] if any one of those drives fail, the entire logical volume is unusable.
[16:38:37] Now we're moving on to an LVM raid.
[16:38:40] And raid is just an acronym for redundant array of independent disks.
[16:38:46] I keep saying LVM raid, as if there was another type of raid option.
[16:38:51] And there is.
[16:38:52] It's called an...
[16:38:53] MD raid or MD slash raid or an MD AD ADM raid, which some people refer to as an MD admin raid.
[16:39:05] A typical MD admin command would start with MD ADM and look like this.
[16:39:12] The MD raid works outside of an LVM environment, so we're done talking about the MD raid for the time being.
[16:39:20] The next thing we're going to look at is the advantages and disadvantages of the LVM RAID 0 that we created in the previous video.
[16:39:31] A RAID 0 is also known as Striped Data because it has two disk drives and the data will be written out or striped in equal amounts to each disc.
[16:39:41] Meaning the first data will be written to one disk and then to the other, then back to the first drive.
[16:39:48] Then back to the other drive again and this pattern continues for a raid zero.
[16:39:54] The idea behind this is that you would get the fastest read and write times when you...
[16:40:00] using two disk drives instead of one.
[16:40:02] Some people say that was true years ago, but disk drives have improved so much that a
[16:40:08] raid zero is no longer needed.
[16:40:10] I've not done any testing on this myself, so I have no idea if that's true or not.
[16:40:15] For now, we're going to say that Raid Zero has better I.O.
[16:40:19] performance, and that will be our first advantage of a Raid Zero.
[16:40:24] The next advantage is that there is no overhead caused by parity controls.
[16:40:29] We'll talk about parity in just a moment or two.
[16:40:32] Then all the disk space can be used.
[16:40:35] And the last advantage is that it is relatively easy to set up.
[16:40:41] The disadvantages are,
[16:40:43] If one drive fails, all the data is lost.
[16:40:47] And then all the disks need to be the same size.
[16:40:51] Moving on to a Rade 1, also known as a mirror,
[16:40:55] uses two or more disks that have the same data.
[16:40:59] on all the disks in the configuration.
[16:41:02] If one of the drives fails, the other drive or drives,
[16:41:06] if there's more than two, can continue on without interruption.
[16:41:10] The advantages of a Rade 1 are read and write speed is good.
[16:41:15] Arrade 0 could be better, as we already discussed.
[16:41:18] Redundant data. If one drive fails,
[16:41:21] the processing can continue with the data on the other drive or drives,
[16:41:26] and that there is no overhead
[16:41:29] caused by parity. The disadvantages are half of the drive space is lost to the redundant copy.
[16:41:35] All data is written two times. And all the disks need to be the same size.
[16:41:41] Now we're moving up to a Raid 5. A Raid 5 is also known as Striping with Parity,
[16:41:48] and it requires a minimum of three disc drives. You can have more.
[16:41:53] When the data is written to a Raid 5,
[16:41:56] it is striped or spread across multiple disks,
[16:41:59] similar to a RAID 0.
[16:42:01] But when it writes the data on one drive,
[16:42:04] it also writes parity data across other drives.
[16:42:07] So the data is not duplicated,
[16:42:10] parity checksum data is created for the Striped data written.
[16:42:14] Creating the parity will slow down the writing of data to the disk drive.
[16:42:18] But if one drive fails, the data can be recreated from that parity data on the other drives.
[16:42:25] So a RAID 5 can continue working if one of the drives fails.
[16:42:30] With RAID 5, we lose one disk drive of disk space for parity,
[16:42:34] meaning if all three disk drives in this example were 10 gigabytes,
[16:42:38] we would only have 20 gigabytes of usable disk space available,
[16:42:43] instead of the 30 gigabytes we would have with an LVM linear configuration.
[16:42:48] That one disk drive of parity disk space is split onto all of the disks in the RAID 5.
[16:42:54] So it doesn't matter which drive fails.
[16:42:57] The raid 5 can continue with a single failed drive,
[16:43:01] which can be replaced and then after replacing that bad disk,
[16:43:05] drive speeds might be a little slower while the data gets populated to that new disk,
[16:43:10] but after that it will be good as new.
[16:43:12] The advantages of a raid 5 are
[16:43:15] read transactions are fast.
[16:43:18] It is fault tolerant.
[16:43:19] We don't say redundant data here because the stripes are written and then
[16:43:24] parity check sums are written.
[16:43:26] So it's not a duplication of data.
[16:43:28] The disadvantages are right transactions are slower
[16:43:33] because when it writes that data,
[16:43:35] it must also calculate the parity check sum.
[16:43:38] You lose one of the disk drives to parity data.
[16:43:41] You might have a temporary loss of performance
[16:43:44] after replacing a drive while it's populating that new disk.
[16:43:48] Now, depending on the disk drive, it could take a long time if it's many gigabytes or terabytes.
[16:43:53] That could take days.
[16:43:55] And then all the disks need to be the same size.
[16:43:59] There is also a Rade 6.
[16:44:02] It's like Rade 5, but has double parity.
[16:44:05] To handle the double parity, it will require a minimum of five disk drives.
[16:44:10] Two of the disk drives are lost to parity, but the Rade will function normally with a loss of up to 2.
[16:44:16] two disk drives at the same time.
[16:44:19] The last one we're going to talk about is the Raid 10.
[16:44:23] Raid 10 is combining a Raid 1 and a Raid 0.
[16:44:28] So first we would create our Raid 0.
[16:44:31] Then we would make a mirror of the Raid 0.
[16:44:35] The advantages of this are fast read and write speed for the Raid 0.
[16:44:40] We have the redundancy of the mirror.
[16:44:42] The disadvantages are...
[16:44:44] It can be expensive. You need a minimum of four disk drives.
[16:44:49] Half of that storage is going to the redundant data or mirroring.
[16:44:54] And then all the disks need to be the same size.

[17:24:29] So what is the advantage of a snapshot over a backup?
[17:24:33] When we take a backup, it backs up everything.
[17:24:37] And when everything is lost, we can restore everything from that backup.
[17:24:42] The problem with backups is that they back up everything,
[17:24:46] and that can take a lot of time to create a backup or restore from a backup,
[17:24:51] especially if we have gigabytes or terabytes of data.
[17:24:55] Snapshots, on the other hand, only store the changes...
[17:24:59] between the current file and the point we want to restore to.
[17:25:03] So that makes taking and restoring snapshots quick and easy.

[18:39:30] In the next tutorial, we will continue looking at ZFS for the Linux command line.
[18:39:37] In previous videos, I talked about ZFS Raid pools.
[18:39:41] I will do a quick review of a ZFS Rade 1 for those that missed that video and a reminder for those that did watch it.
[18:39:49] We said in a ZFS Rade 1, we would lose one disk space of storage,
[18:39:54] meaning if we have 3, 2 gigabyte disks in our Rade 1 using 6.
[18:40:00] 6 gigabytes of data, our RAID 1 would only have 4 gigabytes available for data.
[18:40:05] But if any single drive in that ZFS raid 1 fails, our RAID 1 could continue until that disk could be replaced.
[18:40:15] We then talked about how data is stored on a ZFS raid to handle a disc loss.
[18:40:20] And we learned that it is not data copied around to all the disks as we might think.
[18:40:25] But instead, check-sum information is available on the disks to recreate the data if one of those disks fails.
[18:40:33] The advantage to having check-sum information on that disk is that when we read data from our ZFS raid pool,
[18:40:40] ZFS compares that data to the check-sum information for that data.
[18:40:44] And if that data read does not match the check-sum,
[18:40:48] ZFS can correct that data before it passes that data on.
[18:40:52] So what would cause the data on that disk to go bad?
[18:40:57] The most common reason for data loss is BitRot.
[18:41:00] BitRot happens when you have data sitting on a hard drive.
[18:41:03] Maybe you have a photo that you haven't looked at in years,
[18:41:06] and then you try and open it, and it doesn't show properly or doesn't open at all,
[18:41:11] because some of the bits on that hard drive, where your photo is, have gone bad.
[18:41:16] With ZFS, when we open that picture with some bit rot, the data gets compared to those checksums and gets corrected, so our photo will open as expected.
[18:41:26] Data that gets accessed frequently gets compared to the checksums frequently.
[18:41:31] But what about the data that does not get accessed frequently?
[18:41:35] That is where a Z-pool scrub comes in.
[18:41:39] A scrub will compare all the data in the Z-pool against their checksums.
[18:41:44] and repair any data that does not match up.
[18:41:47] I'm typing Z pool list.
[18:41:50] And we see no Z pools available.
[18:41:52] And I'll type LSBLK to show what discs we have available.
[18:41:57] We see we've got the 5 2 gigabyte disks,
[18:42:00] SDB through SDF, available for ZFS.
[18:42:04] I'll create a ZFS RAID 1 Z pool by pasting in the command.
[18:42:10] Sude create my Raid 1, Raid Z,
[18:42:13] on the discs SDB, SDC, and SDD.
[18:42:19] And let's check the Z-Pool status.
[18:42:23] And everything looks good.
[18:42:24] I'm going to clear the screen.

[18:42:30] To do that, I'll type in S-Poo-ZPool scrub
[18:42:35] and then the name of the Z-Pool that I want to scrub,
[18:42:38] which in this case is my Raid 1.

[18:43:38] Then the suggested action is to replace the faulted drive
[18:43:42] or use Z-Pool Clear to mark the device as repaired.
[18:43:46] So ZFS doesn't tell us we need to replace that disk, but it does warn us that we might want to replace that disk.
[18:43:54] If you see a message like this and you have important data on that disk and that disc is five or more years old,
[18:44:01] you should probably swap out that disk in question for a new one.
[18:44:05] Now I want to point out that scrubs can be resource intensive.
[18:44:09] You probably want to schedule the scrub to run at night or during low activity hours.
[18:44:15] If you started a scrub that will run a long time, then you realize that you're about to go into a busy part of the day.
[18:44:21] You can stop that scrub by adding dash S to the scrub.
[18:44:26] Sudo Z-Pool Scrub-S and then the Z-Pool name.
[18:44:33] It is suggested that we run a scrub at least once a month or more, depending upon how you feel about your data,
[18:44:40] and how long that scrub takes to run on your machine.
[18:44:44] ZFS does have some default Cron's at run.
[18:44:47] They can be found in Etsy CronD ZFS Utils dash Linux.
[18:44:53] Let's less into that file with Sudo,
[18:44:56] less Etsy Cron.D ZFSutils dash Linux.
[18:45:05] We see on the first Sunday of every month,
[18:45:08] ZFS will perform the trim command.
[18:45:10] The trim command will look for all the free space in a Z pool
[18:45:14] and reclaim that free space. Then on the second Sunday of the month, a scrub is run.
[18:45:20] Another thing I should mention is that ZFS does like to use memory, so the more memory you have,
[18:45:27] the better. I have heard people say that if you don't have a lot of memory, it doesn't matter,
[18:45:32] it will just take longer to run. Well, if you only have a couple of gig in your Z-pool,
[18:45:37] you can probably get away with a small amount of memory, but if you're using terabytes of data,
[18:45:42] you will probably want to have as much memory as possible.
[18:45:45] If you only have 4 or 6 gig of memory and you want to work with
[18:45:49] terabytes of data, you may want to consider using LVM instead of ZFS.
[18:45:54] LVM can get by with a lot less memory than ZFS, but you do lose out on that data
[18:46:00] integrity that you get with ZFS.
[18:46:03] Now you may have heard that you need to use ECC memory with ZFS.
[18:46:07] ECC stands for error correction code.
[18:46:10] Yes, it's better to use error correction code memory if you have it, but it's not required for ZFS.
